declare const decode: <T>(schema: Schema.Codec<T, Uint8Array>, content: Uint8Array, context?: {
    readonly objectId?: ObjectId;
    readonly expectedType?: string;
    readonly actualType?: string;
}) => Effect.Effect<T, DecodeError>
```

Decodes BCS `content` bytes with a codec, turning any schema failure into a
`DecodeError` that names the object and the type that was expected.

This is what `Sui.getObject(id, { schema })` does after it has checked the
object's type tag, exposed as `SuiSchema.decode` for the places that already
have bytes: a dynamic field's value, an event payload, a `Stream` of
envelopes an extension decodes itself. Without it every caller reinvents the
same `Schema.decodeUnknownEffect(...).pipe(Effect.mapError(...))`, and the
`DecodeError` it produces is worse than this one.

`expectedType` defaults to the Move type the codec was built with, so passing
it is only needed for a codec that carries none, or to override the recorded
one. `actualType` is the Move type the bytes actually came from, when the
caller knows it — a dynamic field's `name.type`, a stream envelope's `type`:
give it and the same tag check `getObject` does runs here, under the
`typeMatches` rule, before a byte is parsed. `objectId` is recorded on
the error so an operator knows which object did not decode.

**Fails with: `DecodeError`.**

#### `decodeWith` (const)

```ts
declare const decodeWith: <T extends Input, Input, A>(bcsType: BcsType<T, Input>, expectedType: string | undefined, map: (parsed: T) => A) => Schema.Codec<A, Uint8Array>
```

A BCS layout plus the mapping into a domain value, as one codec.

This is the shape every extension writes by hand and writes slightly
differently: parse the bytes with a layout, then hand the raw struct to a
constructor or a mapping function that may throw (an id that has to be
branded, a `bigint` that has to be range-checked, a discriminant that has to
become a union). Written out it is `SuiSchema.bcs(...)` piped into a
`Schema.decodeTo` with a `transformEffect` and an `Effect.try`, and the part
that gets forgotten is turning the thrown value into a schema issue, so the
failure arrives as a defect instead of a `DecodeError`.

`map` is called with whatever the layout parsed. Returning a value decodes
it; **throwing** fails the decode, and the thrown value's message becomes the
`DecodeError.issue` the caller sees, with the expected type already on it.
The result is a `Schema.Codec<A, Uint8Array>` like any other: pass it as
`sui.getObject(id, { schema })`, and the Move type check still runs first
because the annotation `bcs` leaves behind survives the composition.

Encoding is not supported: a mapping function has no inverse, and inventing
one silently is worse than saying so. Encode with the layout itself when you
need bytes back.

**Fails with: `DecodeError` (through the schema), when the bytes do not parse or `map` throws.**

#### `name` (function)

```ts
export declare function name<T>(codec: Schema.Codec<T, Uint8Array>, key: T): DynamicFieldName;
export declare function name<T extends Input, Input>(layout: BcsType<T, Input>, type: string, key: Input): DynamicFieldName;
```

The key of a dynamic field, built from **one** description of it: the Move
type and the BCS bytes that `getDynamicFieldOption`,
`getDynamicObjectFieldOption` and the transaction builder all need, so the
type string and the layout cannot drift apart.

Two forms:

- `name(codec, key)` takes a `SuiSchema.bcs(layout, type)` codec — the type
  comes from the codec — and the key as the codec decodes it (a `u64` field
  is a `string`).
- `name(layout, type, key)` takes the `@mysten/bcs` layout and the Move type,
  and the key in the layout's **input** form, so `{ index: 0 }` is fine for a
  `u64` field. Use it for a primitive key (`name(bcs.u64(), "u64", 7)`).

The type is normalized. Throws when the codec carries no Move type (it was
not built with one) or when the key does not serialize — both are bugs in
the calling code, not runtime conditions.

**Never fails otherwise.**

#### `matchesType` (const)

```ts
declare const matchesType: (expected: string, actual: string) => boolean
```

Whether an object's type tag satisfies an expected type.

One rule, used everywhere a Move type is checked — the BCS bridge, the
`expectedType` option of `Sui.getObject`, `getObjectOption` and `getObjects`,
`SuiSchema.decode`, and the fake's owned-object filter:

- **An expected tag with no type arguments names the generic itself**, so
  `pkg::m::Composition` matches every instantiation:
  `pkg::m::Composition<0x…::share::Share>` included. Only
  `address::module::name` is compared, with the address normalized. This is
  what a node does when a bare type is used as a filter, and it is what makes
  a generic Move type usable with the bridge at all: a codec is written once
  for `Composition<T>`, and the instantiation lives on the object.
- **An expected tag that carries type arguments is compared in full**, after
  `normalizeStructTag`, so `Coin<0x2::sui::SUI>` and its padded spelling are
  the same type and `Coin<0x2::sui::SUI>` does not match `Coin<0x…::usdc::USDC>`.

Anything that is not a struct tag — the literal `package`, a primitive — is
compared as a normalized string. The object keeps its own instantiated type
on `SuiObject.type`; this only decides whether the bytes may be decoded.

**It never throws, which is the reason it is public** as
`SuiSchema.matchesType`. A dynamic field's `name.type` is legally a primitive
— `u64`, `bool`, `address`, `vector<u8>` — and the SDK's own
`normalizeStructTag` throws on every one of them, so the obvious way to
filter a `streamDynamicFields` by key type dies on the first `u64` key.
Comparing tags with this is safe on any string at all.

**Never fails.**

### `SuiService` (interface)

```ts
export interface SuiService {
    /** The network the underlying client was built for. */
    readonly network: SuiClientTypes.Network;
    /**
     * The mechanical tier this `Sui` was built over.
     *
     * `Sui` is the tier application code reads through, but writing a
     * transaction needs `executeTransaction` and the SDK client object behind
     * `use`, neither of which belongs on the opinionated tier. Exposing the core
     * here is what lets every `Tx.*` function declare `R = Sui` and nothing
     * else. Reach for it directly only for a field or method `Sui` does not
     * expose.
     */
    readonly core: SuiCore["Service"];
    /** The genesis checkpoint digest of the chain, read once at layer build. */
    readonly chainId: string;
    /**
     * The chain's own clock, read from the Clock object `0x6` through the BCS
     * bridge and never cached.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly chainTime: Effect.Effect<DateTime.Utc, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Reads one object, decoding its BCS content with `opts.schema` when given.
     *
     * The object's type tag is checked before the bytes are parsed: against
     * `opts.expectedType` when given, otherwise against the type
     * `SuiSchema.bcs` recorded on the codec. This member and the two below are
     * written as overloads, not `Effect.fn`, because `Effect.fn` cannot express
     * an overload set; the implementations are `Effect.fn` and carry the span.
     *
     * A Move **package** is an object too, with `type: "package"` and no Move
     * content: its `content` is an empty `Uint8Array` (the node sends none), and
     * a `schema` read of one fails the type check.
     *
     * Fails with: `ObjectNotFound`, `ObjectDeleted`, `ObjectUnavailable`,
     * `DecodeError`, `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getObject: {
        <S>(id: ObjectId, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<SuiObject<S>, GetObjectError>;
        (id: ObjectId, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<SuiObject<Uint8Array>, GetObjectError>;
    };
    /**
     * Like `getObject`, but a missing or deleted object is `None` rather than a
     * failure.
     *
     * Fails with: `ObjectUnavailable`, `DecodeError`, `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getObjectOption: {
        <S>(id: ObjectId, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<SuiObject<S>>, ObjectUnavailable | DecodeError | TransportError>;
        (id: ObjectId, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<SuiObject<Uint8Array>>, ObjectUnavailable | DecodeError | TransportError>;
    };
    /**
     * Reads many objects, chunked by 50, checking that the node answered for
     * each requested id exactly once. Ids are normalized and deduplicated before
     * the request, so asking twice for the same object is legal and returns the
     * same `Result` twice. Per-object failures are `Result` values.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getObjects: {
        <S>(ids: ReadonlyArray<ObjectId>, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<ReadonlyArray<Result.Result<SuiObject<S>, BatchItemError>>, TransportError>;
        (ids: ReadonlyArray<ObjectId>, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<ReadonlyArray<Result.Result<SuiObject<Uint8Array>, BatchItemError>>, TransportError>;
    };
    /**
     * `getObjects` for a caller who wants the first per-item failure to fail the
     * whole read.
     *
     * The soft idiom — filter or default the `Result` array `getObjects` returns
     * — is right when a missing object is ordinary. This is the hard one: every
     * id must be there, and the first that is not is the failure. Order is the
     * order of `ids`.
     *
     * Fails with: `ObjectNotFound`, `ObjectDeleted`, `ObjectUnavailable`,
     * `DecodeError`, `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.1.2
     */
    readonly getObjectsStrict: {
        <S>(ids: ReadonlyArray<ObjectId>, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<ReadonlyArray<SuiObject<S>>, BatchItemError | TransportError>;
        (ids: ReadonlyArray<ObjectId>, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<ReadonlyArray<SuiObject<Uint8Array>>, BatchItemError | TransportError>;
    };
    /**
     * The balance of one coin type for one owner; `coinType` defaults to SUI.
     *
     * `coinType` is any spelling — a plain `"0x2::sui::SUI"` is fine — and is
     * normalized by the SDK. Fails with: `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getBalance: (owner: SuiAddress, coinType?: CoinType | string) => Effect.Effect<Balance, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every balance an owner holds, one per coin type, paginated.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamBalances: (owner: SuiAddress) => Stream.Stream<Balance, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every coin object of one coin type an owner holds, paginated. `coinType`
     * defaults to SUI, the way the SDK's `listCoins` does; it is the coin type,
     * not the `0x2::coin::Coin<…>` object type, in any spelling (the SDK
     * normalizes it).
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamCoins: (owner: SuiAddress, coinType?: CoinType | string) => Stream.Stream<Coin, TransportError>;
    /**
     * The metadata of a coin type; `None` when the coin type has no metadata
     * object, which is what the node answers for it. `coinType` is any spelling;
     * the SDK normalizes it.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly getCoinMetadataOption: (coinType: CoinType | string) => Effect.Effect<Option.Option<CoinMetadata>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * The address a SuiNS name points at; `None` when the name resolves to
     * nothing, and when the node has no SuiNS for this network (`UNIMPLEMENTED`,
     * which is what a localnet answers).
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly resolveNameServiceAddressOption: (name: string) => Effect.Effect<Option.Option<SuiAddress>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * The default SuiNS name of an address; `None` when it has none.
     *
     * From `@mysten/sui` 2.33.2 the node's "no name" is an answer
     * (`{ data: { name: null } }`); before it, the gRPC transport threw
     * `NOT_FOUND`. Both are `None` here, so the answer does not depend on the SDK
     * version. A node with no SuiNS for this network (`UNIMPLEMENTED`, which is
     * what a localnet answers) is `None` too.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly defaultNameServiceNameOption: (address: SuiAddress) => Effect.Effect<Option.Option<string>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Reads one dynamic field; absence is `None`.
     *
     * `name` is the key — its Move type and BCS bytes; `SuiSchema.name(codec,
     * key)` builds one from the same codec that describes the key. With
     * `opts.schema` the value is decoded too, onto `content`, after its type
     * (`valueType`) is checked against `opts.expectedType` or the type the codec
     * records — the same check `getObject` does, so a forgotten `actualType`
     * cannot skip it. Written as an overload set for the same reason `getObject`
     * is; the implementation is `Effect.fn`.
     *
     * Fails with: `DecodeError` (only with a `schema`), `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getDynamicFieldOption: {
        <S>(parent: ObjectId, name: DynamicFieldName, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<DecodedDynamicField<S>>, DecodeError | TransportError>;
        (parent: ObjectId, name: DynamicFieldName, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<DynamicField>, TransportError>;
    };
    /**
     * Reads the object stored under a dynamic **object** field
     * (`0x2::dynamic_object_field`), decoding its content with `opts.schema`
     * when given; absence is `None`.
     *
     * `name` is the key as the Move code wrote it — its own type and BCS bytes —
     * not the `dynamic_object_field::Wrapper<…>` the SDK wraps it in. The
     * object's type tag is checked the way `getObject` checks it. Written as an
     * overload set for the same reason `getObject` is; the implementation is
     * `Effect.fn`.
     *
     * Fails with: `DecodeError`, `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly getDynamicObjectFieldOption: {
        <S>(parent: ObjectId, name: DynamicFieldName, opts: {
            readonly schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array>;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<SuiObject<S>>, DecodeError | TransportError>;
        (parent: ObjectId, name: DynamicFieldName, opts?: {
            readonly schema?: undefined;
            readonly expectedType?: string;
        }): Effect.Effect<Option.Option<SuiObject<Uint8Array>>, DecodeError | TransportError>;
    };
    /**
     * Reads an executed transaction. A historical transaction that failed on
     * chain fails here too, so there is one representation of an on-chain failure.
     *
     * Fails with: `ExecutionFailed`, `TransactionNotFound`, `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly getTransaction: (digest: Digest) => Effect.Effect<Executed, ExecutionFailed | TransactionNotFound | TransportError>;
    /**
     * Simulates a transaction with the fixed simulate include set.
     *
     * `opts.sender` is set on the transaction when the recipe did not set one.
     * A simulation needs *a* sender: the SDK substitutes the zero address when
     * the transaction carries none, which is what a read-only simulation of a
     * public function wants and is what happens here when `sender` is omitted. A
     * recipe may `tx.setSender(...)` itself, and then it wins. Bytes that are
     * already serialized carry their own sender and `opts.sender` does not apply.
     *
     * Fails with: `SimulationFailed`, `BuildError`, `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly simulate: (input: Recipe | Transaction | Uint8Array, opts?: {
        readonly sender?: SuiAddress;
    }) => Effect.Effect<Simulation, SimulationFailed | BuildError | TransportError>;
    /**
     * Reads a Move return value without executing: simulates with checks disabled
     * and decodes return value `result` (default 0) of command `command`
     * (default the last command).
     *
     * The codec may be a bare `@mysten/bcs` `BcsType` — `bcs.Address` (a value,
     * 32 fixed bytes), `bcs.u64()`, `bcs.vector(bcs.u64())` — because a Move
     * **return value** has no struct tag to check it against. A `SuiSchema.bcs` codec works too, and so does one
     * composed with `Schema.decodeTo(DomainClass, …)`; no type tag is compared
     * either way.
     *
     * `input` is anything `simulate` takes: a recipe, a `Transaction`, or
     * serialized bytes. `opts.sender` is the address the call is simulated as,
     * defaulting to the zero address the way the SDK does; a recipe that sets its
     * own sender wins, and bytes carry their own.
     *
     * Fails with: `SimulationFailed`, `BuildError`, `DecodeError`, `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly view: <S, I>(input: Recipe | Transaction | Uint8Array, schema: Schema.Codec<S, Uint8Array> | BcsType<S, I>, opts?: {
        readonly command?: number;
        readonly result?: number;
        readonly sender?: SuiAddress;
    }) => Effect.Effect<S, SimulationFailed | BuildError | DecodeError | TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every object an address owns, paginated; `opts.type` filters by Move type
     * in any spelling (a bare generic such as `0x2::coin::Coin` matches every
     * instantiation). Fails with: `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly streamOwnedObjects: (owner: SuiAddress, opts?: {
        readonly type?: StructTag | string;
    }) => Stream.Stream<SuiObject<Uint8Array>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every dynamic field of a parent, paginated.
     *
     * There is no key filter: the node has none, so a caller filters the stream
     * on `entry.name.type`. Do that with `SuiSchema.matchesType`, not with the
     * SDK's `normalizeStructTag` — a dynamic-field key is legally a primitive
     * (`u64`, `bool`, `address`, `vector<u8>`) and `normalizeStructTag` throws on
     * every one of them, which turns the first such key in a parent's fields into
     * a defect. The value bytes are decoded with `SuiSchema.decode`, passing
     * `actualType: entry.valueType` so the same tag check `Sui.getObject` does
     * runs before a byte is parsed.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     */
    readonly streamDynamicFields: (parent: ObjectId) => Stream.Stream<DynamicFieldEntry, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every event matching `filter` (all events when omitted), paginated in
     * ledger order — oldest first, or newest first with
     * `{ order: "descending" }`.
     *
     * `filter` is the SDK's `EventFilter`: exactly one of `sender`,
     * `emitModule` (`package::module`) or `eventType` (a module, or a full type;
     * a bare generic matches every instantiation on gRPC and GraphQL). A full
     * `eventType` is normalized here, because gRPC refuses an unpadded address
     * in one. Decode an
     * event's payload with `SuiSchema.decode(codec, entry.bcs, { actualType:
     * entry.eventType })`. The stream follows `hasNextPage`, not the item count,
     * so a scan-limited server's empty pages do not end it early, and it **ends**
     * once it has caught up — `tailEvents` is the one that follows the chain.
     * `opts.after` / `opts.before` resume from a committed cursor; to commit one,
     * read `streamEventPages` instead, which carries it.
     *
     * A malformed filter is a defect carrying the SDK's sentence, not a
     * `TransportError`. Fails with: `TransportError` (an `OUT_OF_RANGE` status
     * means the node has pruned the history the cursor points into).
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamEvents: (filter?: EventFilter, opts?: ListOptions) => Stream.Stream<EventEntry, TransportError>;
    /**
     * `streamEvents` a page at a time, each page carrying the cursor to resume
     * from: commit `page.endCursor` with the page's items and pass it back as
     * `after`.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamEventPages: (filter?: EventFilter, opts?: ListOptions) => Stream.Stream<ListPage<EventEntry>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every event matching `filter`, oldest first, **without end**: once caught
     * up it waits `opts.poll` (one second) and asks again from its last cursor.
     *
     * It survives a node restart: a retryable `TransportError` is retried every
     * `poll` from the last cursor, logged at warning level. On a filtered tail it
     * also keeps its cursor near the ledger head while nothing matches (see
     * `ListPage`), so a pruning node does not prune past it. **That reads the
     * unfiltered head and the filtered page as two requests**, which agree only
     * when they reach the same node (or nodes at the same height): behind a
     * load-balanced endpoint such as a public fullnode, a head from a node that
     * is ahead plus an empty page from one that lags moves the cursor past
     * matches the lagging node had not reached, and they are never read. Point a
     * filtered tail whose completeness matters at a single (sticky) node.
     * `Stream.retry` on
     * the result starts again from `opts.after`, not from where it stopped: for a
     * restartable indexer use `tailEventPages` and commit each page's
     * `endCursor`.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`, only when it is not retryable (a cursor the
     * node has pruned past answers `OUT_OF_RANGE`).
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly tailEvents: (filter?: EventFilter, opts?: TailOptions) => Stream.Stream<EventEntry, TransportError>;
    /**
     * `tailEvents` a page at a time, each page carrying the cursor to resume
     * from. A caught-up filtered tail emits an **empty** page whenever it moves
     * its cursor to the ledger head, so that position can be committed too —
     * with `tailEvents`' caveat: that move is sound only when the head and the
     * page come from one node (or nodes at the same height), so a filtered tail
     * whose completeness matters uses a single (sticky) node, not a
     * load-balanced endpoint.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`, only when it is not retryable.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly tailEventPages: (filter?: EventFilter, opts?: TailOptions) => Stream.Stream<ListPage<EventEntry>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every transaction matching `filter` (all transactions when omitted),
     * paginated in ledger order, oldest first unless `{ order: "descending" }`.
     *
     * `filter` is the SDK's `TransactionFilter`: exactly one of `sender` or
     * `function`. **A function filter must be fully qualified**
     * (`package::module::function`): the SDK cannot continue a `package` or
     * `package::module` filter past its first page, so this refuses one up front,
     * as a defect, rather than failing after fifty transactions. (For a single
     * page of a module filter, call `sui.core.listTransactions` directly.)
     * Each transaction is read with the execute include set and decoded to an
     * `Executed`. A transaction that applied and **failed** is an ordinary
     * entry in a history, so it is a `Result` failure carrying the
     * `ExecutionFailed` — not a failure of the stream, which `getTransaction`'s
     * "a failure is a failure" rule would make it.
     *
     * **`{ affectedAddress }` (0.5.0)** lists every transaction that moved the
     * address's state — incoming transfers as well as its own — through the gRPC
     * ledger service's `affectedAddress` predicate (see
     * `AffectedAddressFilter`). Ordering, `after`/`before` and the cursors are
     * the other filters' exactly: ledger order, oldest first unless
     * descending, fifty digests a page, and an `endCursor` in the SDK's own
     * encoding. Each page is one listing of digests and then one
     * `getTransaction` per digest, so it costs a page's worth of reads; a
     * digest the node prunes between the two is left out of the page. On a
     * transport without the gRPC ledger service it fails with a non-retryable
     * `TransportError` whose `status` is `UNIMPLEMENTED` — never a silent
     * sender-only answer. A pruning node (localnet keeps a few dozen
     * checkpoints) lists only what it still holds.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError` (`status: "UNIMPLEMENTED"` for an
     * `affectedAddress` filter on a transport that cannot serve it).
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamTransactions: (filter?: TransactionFilter | AffectedAddressFilter, opts?: ListOptions) => Stream.Stream<Result.Result<Executed, ExecutionFailed>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * `streamTransactions` a page at a time, each page carrying the cursor to
     * resume from (see `streamEventPages`).
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly streamTransactionPages: (filter?: TransactionFilter | AffectedAddressFilter, opts?: ListOptions) => Stream.Stream<ListPage<Result.Result<Executed, ExecutionFailed>>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Every transaction matching `filter`, oldest first, without end — the
     * transaction twin of `tailEvents`, with the same polling, retry and
     * filtered-cursor behaviour, and `streamTransactions`' rule for function
     * filters. The same caveat holds: a filtered tail advances its cursor to the
     * unfiltered head read just before an empty page, which is sound only when
     * both reads reach one node (or nodes at the same height). Behind a
     * load-balanced endpoint it can skip matches for good, so a filtered tail
     * whose completeness matters uses a single (sticky) node.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`, only when it is not retryable.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly tailTransactions: (filter?: TransactionFilter | AffectedAddressFilter, opts?: TailOptions) => Stream.Stream<Result.Result<Executed, ExecutionFailed>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * `tailTransactions` a page at a time (see `tailEventPages`), with the
     * same caveat: a filtered tail whose completeness matters uses a single
     * (sticky) node, because its move to the head is sound only when the head
     * and the page come from the same ledger height.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`, only when it is not retryable.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly tailTransactionPages: (filter?: TransactionFilter | AffectedAddressFilter, opts?: TailOptions) => Stream.Stream<ListPage<Result.Result<Executed, ExecutionFailed>>, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Serializes work for one sender, so two transactions from the same address
     * cannot pick the same gas coin. Adds no failures of its own.
     */
    readonly withSenderLock: (address: SuiAddress) => <A, E, R>(effect: Effect.Effect<A, E, R>) => Effect.Effect<A, E, R>;
}
```

The opinionated tier over `SuiCore`.

### `TailOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface TailOptions {
    /** Resume strictly after this cursor (a committed `ListPage.endCursor`); `null` is the start. */
    readonly after?: string | null;
    readonly poll?: Duration.Input;
}
```

Where a tail starts and how often it asks once it has caught up.

`after` is a cursor to resume strictly after — a `ListPage.endCursor` the
caller committed — and, omitted, the tail starts at the beginning of the
history the node serves. `poll` is the wait between requests while there is
nothing new, one second unless given.

//         readonly kind: "Unknown" | "ReadOnlyRoot" | "MutateConsensusStreamEnded" | "ReadConsensusStreamEnded" | "Cancelled" | "PerEpochConfig";
//         readonly objectId: ObjectId;
//         readonly version: Version | null;
//         readonly digest: string | null;
//     }[];
//     readonly auxiliaryDataDigest: string | null;
// }
```

Transaction effects. Mirrors `SuiClientTypes.TransactionEffects`.

### `TransactionExpiration` (const)

```ts
declare const TransactionExpiration: Schema
// decodes to:
// {
//     readonly $kind: "None";
//     readonly None: true;
// } | {
//     readonly $kind: "Epoch";
//     readonly Epoch: bigint;
// } | {
//     readonly $kind: "ValidDuring";
//     readonly ValidDuring: {
//         readonly minEpoch: bigint | null;
//         readonly maxEpoch: bigint | null;
//         readonly minTimestamp: bigint | null;
//         readonly maxTimestamp: bigint | null;
//         readonly chain: string;
//         readonly nonce: number;
//     };
// } | {
//     readonly $kind: "Validity";
//     readonly Validity: {
//         readonly allowedProposers: {
//             readonly epoch: bigint;
//             readonly proposers: readonly number[];
//         } | null;
//         readonly minEpoch: bigint | null;
//         readonly maxEpoch: bigint | null;
//         readonly minTimestamp: bigint | null;
//         readonly maxTimestamp: bigint | null;
//         readonly chain: string;
//         readonly nonce: number;
//     };
// }
```

When a transaction stops being valid. Mirrors the SDK's
`TransactionExpiration` enum variant for variant, including its `$kind`
discriminant, so our narrowing and `TransactionDataBuilder`'s agree.

`ValidDuring` is what `Tx.build` sets by default, bounded by **epochs**:
`maxTimestamp` is `null` unless `SubmitConfig.validFor` asks for one, because
no Sui network accepts a timestamp expiration yet. Its `chain` is a replay
guard, so bytes signed for testnet cannot land on mainnet.

### `TransactionFilter` (type)

```ts
export type TransactionFilter = SuiClientTypes.TransactionFilter;
```

A filter on transactions: exactly one of `sender` or `function`. The SDK's
`SuiClientTypes.TransactionFilter`, under its own name.

### `TransactionLookupError` (type)

```ts
export type TransactionLookupError = TransactionNotFound | TransportError;
```

The failures of a transaction lookup.

### `TransactionNotFound` (class)

```ts
export declare class TransactionNotFound extends TransactionNotFound_base {
    readonly digest: Digest;
    /** The one actionable line `SuiError.describe` produces for this error. */
    get message(): string;

```

No transaction with this digest is known to the node.

### `TransferredRef` (interface)

```ts
export interface TransferredRef extends ChangedRef {
    /** The owner the object had going in. */
    readonly inputOwner: Owner;
    /** The owner it has afterwards. */
    readonly owner: Owner;
}
```

A changed object whose **owner changed**: what `Executed.transferred`
and `Simulation.transferred` return. The output side's ref, with the
owner it had going in as `inputOwner` and the one it has now as `owner`.

### `TransportError` (class)

```ts
export declare class TransportError extends TransportError_base {
    readonly cause: unknown;
    readonly method: string;
    readonly retryable: boolean;
    readonly status?: string | undefined;
    /**
     * Builds a `TransportError` out of whatever a call threw, classifying the
     * status and the retryability the way `SuiCore` does for the SDK's own
     * failures.
     *
     * This is for an extension that makes its own network calls — an operator
     * HTTP API, a GraphQL endpoint, a sidecar — and wants its failures to sit on
     * the same axis as the library's: `retryable` read off a gRPC status name, an
     * HTTP status number (5xx and 429), or an abort or timeout, and `status`
     * recorded as the node or the transport spelled it. Hand-building the three
     * fields per call site is how they drift.
     *
     * `retryable` may be forced when the caller knows better than the shape of
     * the cause — an idempotent read that is always safe to repeat, a write that
     * never is. Left out, it is inferred, and inferred conservatively: an
     * unrecognisable cause is **not** retryable.
     *
     * Never fails.
     *
     * @example
     * ```ts
     * import { TransportError } from "@unconfirmed/sui-effect"
     * import { Effect } from "effect"
     *
     * const status = Effect.tryPromise({
     *   try: (signal) => fetch("https://operator.example/status", { signal }),
     *   catch: (cause) => TransportError.fromUnknown("operator.status", cause)
     * })
     * ```
     */
    static readonly fromUnknown: (method: string, cause: unknown, retryable?: boolean) => TransportError;
    /** The one actionable line `SuiError.describe` produces for this error. */
    get message(): string;
}
```

A request did not reach a usable answer. `retryable` is true for the statuses
a read may be retried on: gRPC `UNAVAILABLE`, `DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`,
`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, `INTERNAL` and `UNKNOWN`, HTTP 5xx and 429, and
timeouts. `INTERNAL` and `UNKNOWN` are in the set because the grpc-web
transport reports a refused connection or a DNS failure as `INTERNAL` and an
HTTP 500 as `UNKNOWN`, so without them a node that is merely down is never
retried.

// decodes to:

//     readonly objectId: ObjectId;

Mirrors `SuiClientTypes.UnchangedConsensusObject`.

### `UnexpectedEffects` (class)

```ts
export declare class UnexpectedEffects extends UnexpectedEffects_base {
    readonly digest: Digest;
    readonly expected: string;
    readonly found: readonly ObjectId[];
    /** The one actionable line `SuiError.describe` produces for this error. */
    get message(): string;
}
```

The effects of an applied transaction did not contain what the caller
expected.

Outcome `applied`, and exit 5: this error can only come from an `Executed`,
which means the transaction reached the chain and gas was charged. What is
missing is a receipt, not the transaction. Classifying it `not_applied`
would tell the documented retry idiom to send the caller's intent a second
time for a transaction that already ran.

```ts

// decodes to: Version
```

A Move object version. Encoded as the decimal string the SDK returns.

### `VersionedObject` (type)

```ts
export type VersionedObject = {
    readonly _tag: "Found";
    readonly objectId: string;
    readonly version: string;
    readonly previousTransaction: string | undefined;
} | {
    readonly _tag: "Absent";
    readonly reason: string;

What a versioned object read found: the object as it was at that exact
version, with the digest of the transaction that produced it, or nothing at
all with a reason a log can print.

### `chainOf` (const)

```ts
declare const chainOf: (expiration: TransactionExpiration | undefined) => string | undefined
```

The chain identifier an expiration names, or `undefined` for the variants
that name none (`None`, `Epoch`).

This is the first half of the chain-identity guard `Tx.reconcile` applies
before it asks a node anything: bytes built for one chain must not be
declared expired by another chain's epoch. The second half is
`SignedTransaction.chain`, which `Tx.build` records for the variants that
carry no chain of their own.

**Never fails.**

### `defaultGrpcUrl` (const)

```ts
declare const defaultGrpcUrl: (network: string) => string | undefined
```

The gRPC endpoint sui-effect uses when `SUI_RPC_URL` is not set. The SDK
ships no such table; these are the URLs its own documentation uses.
Returns `undefined` for any other network.

**Never fails.**

### `maxEpochOf` (const)

```ts
declare const maxEpochOf: (expiration: TransactionExpiration | undefined) => bigint | undefined
```

The last epoch in which a transaction can still be applied, or `undefined`
when its expiration sets no such bound (`None`, or a `ValidDuring` with no
`maxEpoch`).

An `Epoch` expiration is that epoch: the SDK's `Epoch` variant means "valid
until the end of this epoch", so it is its own upper bound.

This is the bound that matters in practice. Epochs are what the default
expiration carries and what the validator rule is written in, and unlike a
wall clock an epoch is a consensus fact, so `Tx.reconcile` needs no skew
margin to reason about it.

**Never fails.**

### `maxTimestampMsOf` (const)

```ts
declare const maxTimestampMsOf: (expiration: TransactionExpiration | undefined) => bigint | undefined
```

The wall-clock bound after which a transaction can no longer be applied, in
milliseconds, or `undefined` when its expiration sets no such bound — which
is `None`, `Epoch`, and the default `ValidDuring`, whose `maxTimestamp` is
`null` unless `SubmitConfig.validFor` asks for one.

`Tx.reconcile` compares it against `chainTime` with
`SubmitConfig.expiryMargin` for skew, which the epoch bound needs none of.

**Never fails.**

### `objectRefOf` (const)

```ts
declare const objectRefOf: (ref: ChangedRef) => ObjectRef | undefined
```

The full builder reference of a changed object, when the effects carried
every field the builder needs.

**Never fails.**

### `sdkRefOf` (const)

```ts
declare const sdkRefOf: (ref: ChangedRef | ObjectRef | Coin) => SdkObjectRef | undefined
```

The shape `Transaction#objectRef` and `Inputs.ObjectRef` want: `objectId`
rather than `id`, and a decimal **string** version rather than a `bigint`.

sui-effect's own `ObjectRef` carries the branded `id` and a `bigint`
`version`, because that is what a domain model wants and what a `Version`
check can be run on; the SDK builder wants neither. This is the one
conversion, so nobody writes `{ objectId: ref.id, version: String(ref.version) }`
by hand and gets the field name wrong.

**Only for an address-owned (or immutable) object.** `tx.objectRef` pins a
version, which is exactly right for an owned input and exactly wrong for a
**shared** object: a shared object is passed with `tx.sharedObjectRef({
objectId, initialSharedVersion, mutable })` (the initial shared version lives
on `ref.owner.Shared.initialSharedVersion`), and a **receiving** object with
`tx.receivingRef(...)`, which takes the same three fields this returns.
Passing a shared object by `objectRef` produces bytes a validator rejects.

A `Coin` from `Sui.listCoins` / `Sui.streamCoins` is accepted too
(since 0.4.0): it is an owned object with its id under `objectId`, which is
exactly what a coin passed to `tx.objectRef` or `tx.setGasPayment` needs,
and it always carries a version and a digest, so for a `Coin` the answer is
never `undefined`.

`undefined` when the effects did not carry a version or a digest — a deleted
object has no output version, and nothing can be consumed without both.

**Never fails.**

## `@unconfirmed/sui-effect/tx`


96 exported symbols.

### `Built` (const)

Re-exported from `@unconfirmed/sui-effect`.

### `ClaimViolation` (interface)

```ts
export interface ClaimViolation {
    readonly rule: "outflow" | "recipient" | "object";
    readonly message: string;
}
```

One way the simulated effects stepped outside the claims.

### `ExpirationPolicy` (type)

```ts
export type ExpirationPolicy = "validDuring" | "epoch" | "none";
```

How `Tx.build` sets an expiration when the recipe left one unset.

### `ExpiryEvidencePolicy` (type)

```ts
export type ExpiryEvidencePolicy = "epochThenMiss" | "never";
```

Whether `Tx.reconcile` is allowed to conclude that an expiration window
closed on a transaction the node does not know.

`"epochThenMiss"` is the default and the only rule that produces
`NotApplied { evidence: "expired" }`: the epoch (or timestamp) bound must be
observed as passed, then `getTransaction` must miss, then — after
`SubmitConfig.reconcileRecheck` — both must hold again. A single observation
proves nothing, because a transaction can execute between the lookup and the
expiry check, and a node behind a load balancer can serve an epoch from one
replica and a transaction index from another.

`"never"` disables the rule outright, so an expiration window is never
evidence and an unknown transaction stays `SubmissionUnknown`. That is the
setting for a deployment behind a mixed-node load balancer, where even the
repeated observation can be answered by two different nodes.

**Residual risk, even on `"epochThenMiss"`:** a node whose transaction index
lags its epoch view can report the new epoch and miss a transaction it has in
fact executed, twice in a row. The recheck delay makes that unlikely, not
impossible; a deployment that cannot tolerate it sets `"never"` and settles
unknown submissions by hand.

### `FoldBatch` (interface)

```ts
export interface FoldBatch {
    /** Whose coins these are, and whose address balance receives them. */
    readonly owner: SuiAddress;
    /** The coin type, normalized: the `T` of `0x2::coin::Coin<T>`. */
    readonly coinType: CoinType;
    /** The coins, largest first, at the versions the plan read. */
    readonly coins: ReadonlyArray<Coin>;
    /** Their total, in the coin type's smallest unit. */
    readonly amount: bigint;
    readonly gas: FoldGas;
}
```

One fold transaction's worth of coins: what `fold` turns into a
recipe.

### `FoldGas` (type)

```ts
export type FoldGas = "foldedCoins" | "addressBalance" | "auto";
```

How one fold transaction pays for gas.

- `"foldedCoins"`: SUI only. The batch **is** the gas payment and
  `0x2::coin::send_funds<SUI>(GasCoin, owner)` deposits the whole gas coin,
  so the fold pays for itself and needs no address balance — the bootstrap
  for a fresh, coin-funded account.
- `"addressBalance"`: the coins are inputs and gas comes from the SUI
  address balance (payment `[]`); what a SUI batch too small to cover its
  own budget gets when the balance can.
- `"auto"`: any other coin type. The payment is left to the resolver, which
  pays from the SUI balance when it covers the budget and from SUI coins
  otherwise.

### `FoldMovement` (interface)

```ts
export interface FoldMovement {
    readonly owner: SuiAddress;
    readonly coinType: CoinType;
    /** The batch's coins this fold consumes, at the versions and balances the plan read. */
    readonly consumed: ReadonlyArray<Coin>;
    /** The batch's coins the effects do **not** consume. Empty for a fold that does what it says. */
    readonly missing: ReadonlyArray<Coin>;
    /** The total of `consumed`, in the coin type's smallest unit. */
    readonly fromCoins: bigint;
    /**
     * What the owner's address balance of this coin type gains: `netChange +
     * fromCoins`. For a SUI batch paying for itself, the coins less the net gas
     * (more than the coins when the storage rebates exceed the gas).
     */
    readonly toAddressBalance: bigint;
    /** The owner's net balance change for the coin type, coins and balance together. */
    readonly netChange: bigint;
    /**
     * Whether `toAddressBalance` is exact: every coin of the batch is consumed,
     * and the owner ends up with no coin object of this type created or
     * mutated, so nothing but the address balance can hold the difference.
     */
    readonly exact: boolean;
}
```

What one fold moves, read off its simulation (or its execution):
`consumed`, the batch's coins the effects delete, with the balances the plan
read them at; `fromCoins`, their total; `toAddressBalance`, what the owner's
address balance of the coin type gains; and `netChange`, the owner's net
balance change, which is all `balanceChanges` reports.

### `FoldPlan` (interface)

```ts
export interface FoldPlan {
    readonly owner: SuiAddress;
    /** SUI batches first: their deposits fund the gas of everything after them. */
    readonly batches: ReadonlyArray<FoldBatch>;
    /**
     * SUI coins worth less together than the estimated budget of folding them,
     * with no address balance to pay for it either. Folding them would cost more
     * than they hold, or could not be paid for at all.
     */
    readonly skipped: ReadonlyArray<Coin>;
}
```

What `planFold` answers: the transactions that fold an owner's coins,
in the order they must run, and the SUI dust that cannot pay for its own
fold.

### `FoldResult` (interface)

```ts
export interface FoldResult {
    readonly plan: FoldPlan;
    readonly executed: ReadonlyArray<Executed>;
}
```

What `foldCoins` did: the plan it ran, and one `Executed` per batch, in
order.

### `FromMnemonicOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface FromMnemonicOptions {
    /** The scheme to derive. Default `"ED25519"`, which is what every Sui wallet derives first. */
    readonly scheme?: KeyScheme;
    /**
     * The account index on the scheme's standard path, `[0, 2^31)`. Default 0.
     * Ignored when `path` is given.
     */
    readonly account?: number;
    /**
     * An explicit derivation path, checked against the scheme's shape:
     * `m/44'/784'/a'/c'/i'` (Ed25519, every level hardened, SLIP-0010),
     * `m/54'/784'/a'/c/i` (Secp256k1) or `m/74'/784'/a'/c/i` (Secp256r1).
     */
    readonly path?: string;
}
```

Options of `Signer.fromMnemonic`.

### `FromSecretKeyOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface FromSecretKeyOptions {
    /**
     * The scheme of a key that carries none: raw bytes or a 64-digit hex seed
     * (default `"ED25519"`). For a Bech32 `suiprivkey1…` the key's own flag
     * decides, and a `scheme` that contradicts it is refused.
     */
    readonly scheme?: KeyScheme;
}
```

Options of `Signer.fromSecretKey`.

### `GasPaymentPolicy` (type)

```ts
export type GasPaymentPolicy = "preferAddressBalance" | "requireAddressBalance";
```

How `Tx.build` treats a gas payment the recipe left unset.

- `"preferAddressBalance"` (the default): the resolver decides, and every
  SDK resolver pays from the payer's SUI address balance when it covers the
  budget and the transaction does not use `tx.gas`, and from coins
  otherwise.
- `"requireAddressBalance"`: the payment is pinned to the address balance
  (`[]`) before the build, unless the transaction uses `tx.gas`, and the
  build fails with `BuildError` when the balance cannot cover the budget or
  the resolver paid with coin objects. Fold coins first with
  `Tx.foldCoins`.

A payment the recipe set itself is the recipe's choice under either policy.

### `Gate` (const)

```ts
declare const Gate: Context.Reference<GateService> & {
    defaults: GateService;
    layer: (overrides: Partial<GateService>) => Layer.Layer<never>;
    with: (overrides: Partial<GateService>) => <A, E, R>(effect: Effect.Effect<A, E, R>) => Effect.Effect<A, E, R>;
}
```

The intent policy and its screen.

Because this is a `Context.Reference`, `Tx.verify` keeps `R = Sui`: with
nothing provided the claims are checked and the verdict is `Unscreened`.
`Gate.layer(overrides)` spreads `defaults`; `Gate.with(overrides)`
merges over the gate in force.

### `GateService` (interface)

```ts
export interface GateService {
    /**
     * The semantic screen. It answers in probabilities and decides nothing.
     *
     * Fails with: `TransportError`, which is what `GateService.onUnavailable`
     * is about.
     */
    readonly screen?: (request: ScreenRequest) => Effect.Effect<Screening, TransportError>;
    /**
     * Whether `Tx.run` refuses a transaction that declares no intent, with
     * `PolicyDenied { rule: "intent.required" }`, before it builds anything.
     * Off by default; on is the setting for a wallet an agent drives. It binds
     * `Tx.run` only: the explicit lifecycle (`Tx.build`, `Tx.sign`, `Tx.submit`)
     * is not gated, and a caller assembling it calls `Tx.verify` itself.
     */
    readonly requireIntent: boolean;
    /**
     * What a screen that could not answer means. `"deny"` refuses the transaction
     * with `PolicyDenied { rule: "gate.unavailable" }`: a gate that was configured
     * and is down is not a gate that passed. `"unscreened"` lets the claims check
     * stand alone and records why on the verdict.
     */
    readonly onUnavailable: "deny" | "unscreened";
    /** An effect line the summary covers with less probability than this is refused. */
    readonly minEffectInScope: number;
    /** A claim line the summary has less use for than this is refused. */
    readonly minClaimInScope: number;
    /** A summary less specific than this (0 to 2) covers nothing and is refused. */
    readonly minSpecificity: number;
    /** A summary more likely than this to be addressing the screen is refused. */
    readonly maxInjection: number;
}
```

Every decision `Tx.verify` and `Tx.run` read from context about intents.

### `Intent` (const)

```ts
declare const Intent: Schema
// decodes to:
// {
//     readonly summary: string;
//     readonly author: "user" | "task" | "dapp";
//     readonly claims: {
//         readonly outflows: readonly {
//             readonly coinType: CoinType;
//             readonly max: bigint;
//         }[];
//         readonly recipients: readonly SuiAddress[];
//         readonly objects: readonly ObjectId[];
//     };
// }
```

A declared intent: one sentence for the screen, exact claims for the code.

`Intent.fromSimulation(simulation: Simulation, parties: Parties, opts: {
summary: string; author: IntentAuthor }): Intent` renders a simulation as
the exact claims it implies: every coin the sender loses at exactly the
amount lost (net of gas), every address other than the sender that receives
a coin or an object, every non-coin object that leaves the sender. So
`checkClaims(Intent.fromSimulation(s, p, opts), s, p)` is empty. It is how a
wallet shows a dapp's bytes as claims a person can approve, then holds what
it signs to them with `Tx.verify`; `parties` may be the `Built` itself.

**Never fails; the summary is checked by `Tx.verify` (`intent.invalid`).**

### `IntentAuthor` (const)

```ts
declare const IntentAuthor: Schema
// decodes to: "user" | "task" | "dapp"
```

Who wrote the intent. Provenance for the audit record, and nothing else: the
checks are the same for all three, because none of them can be verified from
here.

`"user"` is a person describing their own transaction. `"task"` is the scope
a person gave an agent **before** the agent built anything, which is the only
form that constrains a prompt-injected agent; an agent describing its own PTB
after the fact is `"dapp"` in all but name. `"dapp"` is a third party's
description of a transaction it built, which is a claim to check and never
evidence.

### `IntentClaims` (const)

```ts
declare const IntentClaims: Schema
// decodes to: { readonly outflows: readonly { readonly coinType: CoinType; readonly max: bigint; }[]; readonly recipients: readonly SuiAddress[]; readonly objects: readonly ObjectId[]; }
```

What the transaction is allowed to do to the sender, deny by default: a coin
that is not listed may not decrease, an address that is not listed may not
receive anything, and an object that is not listed may not leave.

Gas is not an outflow. It is bounded by `SubmitConfig.maxGasBudget`, and
counting it would make every claim a guess about the reference gas price.

### `Journal` (const)

```ts
declare const Journal: Context.Reference<JournalService> & {
    /** A fresh in-memory journal, for a test or a process that wants its own. Never fails. */
    layerMemory: Layer.Layer<never, never, never>;
    /** The in-memory implementation, for building one directly. Never fails. */
    makeMemoryUnsafe: () => JournalService;
}
```

The submission journal.

Being a `Context.Reference`, it is never in an `R`: `Tx.submit` reads it from
context and finds the in-memory default unless something provided another.

**The default is process-wide.** A `Context.Reference`'s default value is
computed once and cached on the reference itself, so every fiber that does
not provide one shares a single `Map` for the life of the process. That is
what makes a one-shot script work with zero wiring, and it is also why a
test that runs `Tx.submit` or `Tx.run` should provide
`Journal.layerMemory`: without it, entries from one test are visible to
the next, and `listUnresolved` returns other tests' submissions.
