**Never fails.**

### `maxEpochOf` (const)

Re-exported from `@unconfirmed/sui-effect`.

### `maxTimestampMsOf` (const)

Re-exported from `@unconfirmed/sui-effect`.

### `planFold` (const)

```ts
declare const planFold: (opts: {
    readonly owner: SuiAddress;
    /** One coin type to fold; every coin type the owner holds coins of when omitted. */
    readonly coinType?: CoinType;
    /** At most this many coins per transaction: 1 to 500, 500 by default. */
    readonly maxCoinsPerTransaction?: number;
}) => Effect.Effect<FoldPlan, TransportError, Sui>
```

Plans the transactions that fold an owner's coin objects into its address
balance. Reads only; nothing is built or signed.

It reads the owner's coins of `coinType` — or, when `coinType` is omitted, of
every coin type the owner holds coins of — the owner's SUI address balance
and the reference gas price. Coins are folded largest first, at most
`maxCoinsPerTransaction` per transaction (default and maximum
`MAX_FOLD_COINS`; values outside 1 to 500 are clamped).

**SUI pays for its own fold** (`gas: "foldedCoins"`), so an account whose
SUI is all coins — a localnet faucet's, say — folds with no address balance
at all. A SUI batch worth less than its estimated budget,
`referenceGasPrice × (10,000 + 1,000 × coins)` MIST, is paid from the
address balance instead (`gas: "addressBalance"`) when the balance projected
after the earlier batches covers that estimate, and is `skipped` otherwise.
Other coin types fold with `gas: "auto"`, after every SUI batch, so the SUI
just folded pays for them.

Preview a batch with `Tx.prepare(Tx.fold(batch), { sender })`, nothing
signed, and read what it moves with `foldMovement`; pass the plan
you previewed to `Tx.foldCoins({ plan, approve })` to run exactly it.

**Fails with: `TransportError`.**

### `prepare` (const)

```ts
declare const prepare: (input: Built | Transaction | Recipe, opts: {
    readonly sender: SuiAddress;
    readonly gasOwner?: SuiAddress;
    /** What the transaction is declared to do; `verified` is present when given. */
    readonly intent?: Intent;
    /** A session bound for what is built here (`Signer.maxEpoch`); see `Tx.build`. Ignored for a `Built`. */
    readonly maxEpoch?: bigint;
}) => Effect.Effect<Prepared, TransportError | SimulationFailed | BuildError | PolicyDenied, Sui>
```

Build (or take) a transaction, simulate exactly its bytes, run
`SubmitConfig.preflight` and, given an intent, `Tx.verify`'s checks — and
sign nothing. The front half of `Tx.run`, for previews, dry runs, sign-only
flows and transactions a dapp hands over; the answer is a `Prepared`
to show a person, then `Tx.sign` (`prepared.verified ?? prepared.built`)
and `Tx.submit`, `Tx.submitVia`, or another party's co-signature.

**A `Built` is not rebuilt.** Bytes from `Tx.fromBytes` — a dapp's request,
a sponsor's reply — are what is simulated and what will be signed;
`opts.sender` must be the sender the bytes name and `opts.gasOwner`, when
given, their payer, or it fails with `PolicyDenied` (`prepare.sender`,
`prepare.gasOwner`) before anything is asked of the node. A recipe or a
`Transaction` goes through `Tx.build` with `sender`, `gasOwner` and
`maxEpoch`, and when the build already simulated exactly the bytes it
returned (`built.simulation`), that simulation is the one used.

**One simulate.** On gRPC the build's resolver simulates a pre-budget
transaction, so a prepare costs that plus one simulate of the final bytes;
off gRPC with a preset budget the build's own simulate is reused and the
prepare costs one in all.

**No sender lock**, unlike `Tx.run`: the answer is a value that may wait for
a person, a second device or a sponsor for as long as it likes, and a lock
held across that would stall every other transaction from the account. The
cost is the one `Tx.build` already documents — two prepares from one sender
can resolve the same gas coin or owned object, and the second to land fails
— so a flow that signs straight after approval uses `Tx.run({ approve })`,
which runs this same front half under the lock exactly when the bytes pin.

**`Gate.requireIntent` does not apply**: it binds `Tx.run`, the call that
signs. A prepare without an intent is how a wallet first sees a dapp's
effects, to render them (`Intent.fromSimulation`) and ask; give the intent
then — here, to `Tx.verify`, or to `Tx.run` — and `verified` is what gets
signed.

**Fails with: `BuildError`, `SimulationFailed`, `PolicyDenied` (the gas ceiling, `preflight`, the intent's rules, `prepare.sender`, `prepare.gasOwner`), `TransportError`.**

### `reconcile` (const)

```ts
declare const reconcile: (input: ReconcileInput) => Effect.Effect<Executed, ExecutionFailed | SubmissionUnknown | NotApplied, Sui>
```

Finds out what happened to a transaction that was sent but never answered
for.

A transaction the node knows is `Executed`, or `ExecutionFailed` when it
applied and aborted. A transaction the node does not know is only ever
`NotApplied` on evidence, and there are exactly two kinds:

- `"expired"`, under an **ordered and repeated** rule, because a closed
  expiration window proves only that the bytes cannot execute *later*, not
  that they did not execute *earlier*, and a transaction can execute between
  a lookup and an expiry check. So: the window must be observed closed, then
  `getTransaction` must miss, then — after `SubmitConfig.reconcileRecheck`
  (two seconds by default, through the `Clock`) — both must hold again. Any
  other order, or a single observation, is `SubmissionUnknown`. Set
  `SubmitConfig.expiryEvidence: "never"` to disable the rule entirely, which
  is what a deployment behind a mixed-node load balancer wants. **Positive
  evidence vetoes it**: when a pinned object names this digest as its last
  mutation the transaction applied, whatever `getTransaction` said, and the
  answer is the receipt or `SubmissionUnknown` — never `expired`. **A miss
  only counts on a node that still holds the window**: on gRPC the node's
  `lowestAvailableCheckpoint` must be at or before the first checkpoint of
  the window's `minEpoch`, or the answer is `SubmissionUnknown` naming the
  pruning. Transports that do not report their history (JSON-RPC, GraphQL)
  cannot be checked; behind a pruning node on those, set `expiryEvidence:
  "never"`. The residual risk is a node whose transaction index lags its
  epoch view;
- `"inputConsumed"` when the transaction that last mutated a pinned object is
  a **different** one **and its own effects report `inputVersion` equal to
  the version these bytes pinned**, so those exact bytes can never execute
  again.

An input that merely advanced is not evidence: the transaction being
reconciled is itself the likeliest thing to have advanced it, and calling
that `NotApplied` would tell the documented retry idiom to execute the
caller's intent a second time. When the live object names *our* digest the
transaction applied and `getTransaction` is asked again; when the consuming
transaction took a **later** version than ours — which is the common case,
because Sui stamps every output with the transaction's Lamport version and
the object "one version on" from ours usually never existed — nothing is
proven and the answer is `SubmissionUnknown`, which carries the bytes so a
later process, or a person, can settle it. Every pinned reference is tried
before that answer is given.

**Chain identity is checked before anything is asked.** Bytes built for one
chain must never be declared expired by another chain's epoch, which a
process-wide journal holding two networks' submissions makes easy to do. A
mismatch is `SubmissionUnknown` naming both chains.

**No `TransportError` escapes.** A recovery read that fails says nothing
about whether the transaction applied, and `SuiError.outcome` puts
`TransportError` on `"not_applied"` — which would tell a wrapper to retry a
submission whose outcome is genuinely unknown. Every read failure here
becomes `SubmissionUnknown` carrying the digest, the bytes and the cause.

**An answer for another digest is not trusted**, here as on the send side:
a `getTransaction` that answers an `Executed` or `ExecutionFailed` for a
different transaction is `SubmissionUnknown` naming both digests, and a
consuming transaction's answer for a different digest is not
`inputConsumed` evidence.

Given only a `Digest` there can be no evidence, so an unknown transaction is
always `SubmissionUnknown`. Pass the `Signed` bytes (or the
`SubmissionUnknown` that carries them) to get the evidence rules.

**A terminal answer is journalled** — `Executed`, `Failed` or `NotApplied`
replaces the entry — when the journal holds this digest unresolved. A digest
the journal has never seen is not written, and `SubmissionUnknown` leaves the
entry as it was. To re-send bytes that are still unknown, use
`resubmit`.

**Fails with: `ExecutionFailed`, `NotApplied`, `SubmissionUnknown`.**

### `reconcileAll` (const)

```ts
declare const reconcileAll: (opts?: {
    /**
     * Re-send, as identical bytes, every entry that is still
     * `SubmissionUnknown` after reconciling. Defaults to `false`.
     *
     * @since 0.4.0
     */
    readonly resubmit?: boolean;
} | undefined) => Effect.Effect<readonly ({
    readonly _tag: "ExecutionFailed";
    readonly error: ExecutionFailed;
} | {
    readonly _tag: "SubmissionUnknown";
    readonly error: SubmissionUnknown;
} | {
    readonly _tag: "NotApplied";
    readonly error: NotApplied;
} | {
    readonly _tag: "Executed";
    readonly executed: Executed;
})[], JournalError, Sui>
```

Settles every unresolved entry in the journal: the explicit startup call a
long-lived application makes after building a durable `Journal`.

**Only unresolved entries come back.** `Signed` and `Unknown` are the tags
that still need an answer; a digest that already settled is not among the
journal's unresolved entries and is not in this array. Ask about one of those
with `recorded`.

Nothing here fails per entry: each one settles to an `Executed`, an
`ExecutionFailed`, a `NotApplied` or a `SubmissionUnknown`, in the order the
journal listed them, and the journal is updated to match. Every settled entry
gets the same evidence rules `Tx.reconcile` applies — the ordered, repeated
expiry rule, the chain-identity guard and the versioned consumer check — so a
startup never reports a transaction that applied as `NotApplied`, and a
recovery read that fails becomes that entry's `SubmissionUnknown` rather than
escaping as a `TransportError` the taxonomy would call "not applied".

**`{ resubmit: true }` re-sends what is still unknown.** An entry that
reconciles to `SubmissionUnknown` is sent again as its identical signed
bytes, exactly as `resubmit` sends them — not when its validity window
has closed or it belongs to another chain — and settles to whatever that
send answers. Without it, an entry with no owned inputs can only be
`SubmissionUnknown` until its window closes, and a program that reconciles
before sending anything new is stuck that long.

The whole call fails only if the journal itself cannot be **read**: a write
that fails after an entry has been settled is logged and the answer stands,
the same rule `Tx.submit` follows.

**Fails with: `JournalError`.**

### `recorded` (const)

```ts
declare const recorded: (digest: Digest) => Effect.Effect<Option.Option<JournalEntry>, JournalError, never>
```

What the journal recorded for one digest, if anything.

`Tx.reconcileAll` returns **only the entries that were still unresolved**,
because those are the ones it had work to do about; a transaction that had
already settled — executed, failed, or proven never applied — is not in its
answer and never will be. This is how to ask about one of those: the entry is
`Executed`, `Failed` or `NotApplied` for a settled digest, `Signed` or
`Unknown` for one still in flight, and `None` for a digest this journal has
never seen (including every digest at all, when the journal is the in-memory
default and the process restarted).

It is exactly `(yield* Journal).get(digest)`, named so that the recovery path
does not have to reach for the reference.

**Fails with: `JournalError`.**

### `remote` (const)

```ts
declare const remote: (signer: RemoteSigner) => Signer
```

Builds a signer around something that signs elsewhere: a KMS, a wallet, a
hardware device, another process.

The returned signer decodes whatever the remote produced, so a malformed
signature is a `SigningError` rather than a surprise at execution. When
`signPersonalMessage` is not given, asking for one fails with `SigningError`
instead of pretending.

**Never fails.**

### `resubmit` (const)

```ts
declare const resubmit: (input: SubmissionUnknown | Signed) => Effect.Effect<Executed, SubmitError, Sui>
```

Re-sends signed bytes whose outcome is unknown — the **identical** bytes,
never a rebuild — and settles them the way `submit` does.

Safe by construction: the bytes have one digest, and Sui executes a digest at
most once, so a re-send can only land the transaction that was already
signed, or tell you it already landed. This is the way out of a
`SubmissionUnknown` that `reconcile` cannot settle yet — a submission with no
owned inputs whose evidence can only be the expiry window, a node that lost
the request — without waiting for that window to close.

What it does **not** do is send bytes that cannot help:

- a digest the journal already holds as **settled** is not sent again:
  `Executed` or `Failed` is read back with `reconcile`, and `NotApplied`
  fails with that `NotApplied`;
- bytes built for another chain than this `Sui`'s are not sent; the
  reconcile names both chains;
- bytes whose validity window has observably closed are not sent; they are
  reconciled, which is where the expiry rule decides.

Unlike a first `Tx.submit`, a refusal (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`) on the re-send is
**not** taken as "nothing executed": an earlier send of the same bytes may
have landed, so the refusal reconciles like any other ambiguous answer.

Given a `SubmissionUnknown` that carries no bytes (one that came from
reconciling a bare digest), there is nothing to send and this is `reconcile`.

**Fails with: `ExecutionFailed`, `NotApplied`, `SubmissionUnknown`, `JournalError` (reading the journal, or the `Signed` write before the send), `TransportError` (in the union for `SubmitError`'s sake; a re-send never fails with one).**

### `run` (const)

```ts
declare const run: <E = never>(recipe: Transaction | Recipe, opts: {
    readonly signer: Signer;
    readonly gasOwner?: SuiAddress;
    /**
     * The gas owner's signer, for a sponsored transaction. Required whenever
     * the bytes name a gas owner that is not the sender.
     */
    readonly sponsor?: Signer;
    /**
     * Called with the signed bytes **after every signature is on them and
     * before the first `executeTransaction`**, which is the one moment a
     * consumer's own record has to be written: the digest is final from here
     * on, and anything that happens next may have reached the network.
     *
     * `Tx.submit` already writes its `Signed` journal entry at this point; this
     * is for the record the journal does not hold — a domain row joining the
     * digest to a batch, an outbox, a log line an operator greps. It runs
     * inside the sender lock, so it is ordered with the submission it belongs
     * to.
     *
     * Failing it fails the run **before anything is sent**, which is why its
     * error is a `JournalError`: that is the taxonomy's "the record could not
     * be written and nothing has gone out yet", it is already in `Tx.run`'s
     * union, and it is `not_applied`, so the documented retry idiom is correct.
     * Map your own persistence failure into it
     * (`Effect.mapError((cause) => new JournalError({ cause }))`).
     *
     * @since 0.1.2
     */
    readonly onSigned?: (signed: Signed) => Effect.Effect<void, JournalError>;
    /**
     * What this transaction is declared to do. When present, the built bytes
     * go through `verify` before anything is signed, sharing its
     * simulate with `SubmitConfig.preflight`. `Gate.requireIntent` makes it
     * mandatory.
     *
     * @since 0.5.0
     */
    readonly intent?: Intent;
    /**
     * Shown the `Prepared` transaction — the built bytes with their gas,
     * the simulation of exactly those bytes, the verified intent — **after**
     * `preflight` and the intent checks and **before the first signature**.
     * Succeeding approves; failing refuses with the caller's own error (a
     * wallet's `UserRejected`), which `Tx.run` fails with, nothing signed and
     * nothing journalled. It runs where signing runs: under the sender lock
     * when the bytes pin something, after it is released when they do not,
     * so a person taking their time never holds an address-balance account's
     * lock. Giving it always costs the simulate it is shown (the build's own,
     * when the build made one of exactly these bytes).
     *
     * @since 0.5.0
     */
    readonly approve?: (prepared: Prepared) => Effect.Effect<void, E>;
}) => Effect.Effect<Executed, TransportError | SimulationFailed | ExecutionFailed | SubmissionUnknown | NotApplied | SigningError | BuildError | PolicyDenied | JournalError | E, Sui>
```

Build, preflight, sign and submit, under the sender lock wherever two runs
could race.

Owned objects and gas coins are resolved during build, so two transactions
from one address that overlap can pin the same one at the same version and
one of them will fail on chain. Under `SubmitConfig.lockSender: "whenPinned"`
(the default) `Tx.run` holds the sender lock for **every build**, and on
through signing and submission **only when the built bytes pin** an owned or
receiving input or carry any gas payment — a gas coin **or a coin
reservation**. Bytes that pin nothing — an empty payment (gas from an address
balance, drawn at execution), inputs that are pure values, shared objects or
withdrawals — are signed and sent with the lock released, so address-balance
transactions from one sender run concurrently, serialized only for the length
of a build. The span attribute `sui.sender_lock` says which happened
(`"held"`, `"build"`; `"none"` under `"never"`). `"always"` holds the lock
from build through submit on every run (the rule before 0.4.0), `"never"`
takes none. This is the whole reason to prefer `Tx.run` over calling the
steps separately.

**A coin reservation reserves the payer's whole SUI address balance as of the
build.** That is what the resolver gives a recipe that uses `tx.gas` when its
payer has an address balance (on an account with no SUI coins, the payment is
the reservation alone). Any spend from that balance landing between the build
and the send makes the node refuse it at submit (`INVALID_ARGUMENT`, "Invalid
withdraw reservation", which escapes as `TransportError`), and one in flight
beside it makes one of them execute as `InsufficientFundsForWithdraw`. So such
a run holds the lock through submission, and a run whose recipe uses `tx.gas`
first waits — holding its locks, before it builds — until no lock-free run is
in flight on its payer (the gas owner when it is someone else, else the
sender). A lock-free run counts as in flight on its sender and its gas owner
from just before it releases the lock until it settles, fails or is
interrupted. Residual: a reservation that appears only after the build — an
SDK intent such as `tx.coin({ balance })` that fell back to splitting
`tx.gas` — holds the lock but did not wait, and a sponsored address-balance
run (which takes only its sender's lock) can still start drawing on its
sponsor's balance while that sponsor's own `tx.gas` run builds. A flow that
must never see either uses `lockSender: "always"`.

Lock-free runs can together withdraw more than one balance holds; the node
then executes the one that overdraws as a failure (`InsufficientFundsForWithdraw`,
no gas charged), which is `ExecutionFailed`. A flow that spends a balance
down to zero uses `lockSender: "always"`.

The address whose coins may be spent is the **gas owner** when there is one:
two sponsored runs for different senders paid by one sponsor are exactly the
case that picks the same coin twice. When sender and gas owner differ, both
locks are taken, in ascending address order — a fixed order, so two runs that
each need the same pair cannot deadlock by taking them the other way round —
**except when the recipe pays from an address balance**: an explicit empty
payment, which `Tx.sponsored` sets, selects no coin, so the gas owner's lock
is not taken and one sponsor serves many senders at once.

When `SubmitConfig.preflight` is set it costs one extra simulate, and is
where spend limits and target policies refuse a transaction before anything
is signed.

**`intent` puts the bytes through `verify` before anything is
signed**, on the same simulate `preflight` uses — one extra simulate for
either or both, run after the build exactly where signing runs (under the
lock when the bytes pin). `Gate.requireIntent` makes it mandatory: a run
that declares none fails with `PolicyDenied { rule: "intent.required" }`
before the recipe is even applied.

**A sponsored run needs both signatures.** A transaction whose gas owner is
not its sender is signed by *both* parties; one signature is bytes a
validator rejects. So when `opts.gasOwner` differs from the signer's address
— or when the recipe itself set a different gas owner, which `Tx.sponsored`
does — `opts.sponsor` is required and co-signs the same bytes. Without it
`Tx.run` fails with `SigningError` naming the address whose signature is
missing, before anything is built (the recipe is applied first, so a gas
owner it sets is known by then too). Use the
explicit lifecycle (`Tx.build`, `Tx.sign`, `Tx.cosign`, `Tx.submit`) when the
two parties cannot both sign in one process.

**The locks are chosen from the recipe itself.** The recipe is applied to a
fresh `Transaction` before any lock is taken, so a gas owner it sets itself
(`Tx.sponsored` does) and the gas payment it leaves are known when the locks
are chosen — passing the same address as `opts.gasOwner` changes nothing.
The recipe runs outside the lock: it only describes commands, and anything
it reads from the chain belongs in the resolver, which runs inside.

**`onSigned` is the hook between signing and sending.** A program with its
own record to keep — a batch row, an outbox, an idempotency key — has to
write the digest before the first send, and that used to mean giving up
`Tx.run` and reassembling `withSenderLock(build → sign → record → submit)` by
hand. Pass `onSigned` instead: it runs inside the sender lock, after the last
signature and before `Tx.submit`'s first `executeTransaction`, and failing it
fails the run with nothing sent.

**`approve` is the person in the loop** (0.5.0). After `preflight` and the
intent, before the first signature, `approve` is shown the `Prepared`
transaction — the same thing `prepare` answers — and refuses by
failing with its own error, which is then `Tx.run`'s: nothing is signed,
`onSigned` does not run and nothing is journalled. It runs under the lock
exactly when signing does. A sponsored run approves too: `built.gasOwner`
and `built.gas.owner` name the sponsor.

**The session bound** (0.5.0). A signer with a `maxEpoch` (a zkLogin
session) — and a sponsor with one — bounds the window the build sets:
`Tx.build`'s `maxEpoch` is the earlier of the two, and a bound already below
the current epoch fails with `SigningError { reason: "expired" }` before
anything resolves.

**Fails with: `BuildError`, `SimulationFailed`, `PolicyDenied`, `SigningError`, `ExecutionFailed`, `NotApplied`, `SubmissionUnknown`, `JournalError`, `TransportError` (from the build reads; once bytes are sent, transport failures become `SubmissionUnknown`), and `E` — whatever `approve` fails with.**

### `sendFunds` (const)

```ts
declare const sendFunds: (opts: {
    readonly coinType?: CoinType;
    readonly amount: bigint;
    readonly recipient: SuiAddress;
    /** Whether SUI the balance does not cover may be split from the gas coin. Default `true`. */
    readonly useGasCoin?: boolean;
}) => Recipe
```

Sends `amount` of a coin type into `recipient`'s **address balance**:
`0x2::balance::send_funds<T>(tx.balance({ type, balance: amount }), recipient)`,
the canonical transfer on an address-balance account.

The amount comes from the sender's address balance when that covers it — a
`FundsWithdrawal`, no versioned input at all, so with gas from the balance
too the transaction pins nothing. Otherwise it comes from coins: for SUI,
split from the gas coin, the SDK's default; for another type, the sender's
coins merged and split, the remainder returned to its balance. The recipient
receives a balance rather than a coin object it would have to fold.
`coinType` defaults to SUI; `amount` is in the type's smallest unit.

`useGasCoin` is the SDK's option of that name and only matters for SUI that
the balance does not cover. Leave it `true` for the sender's own
transactions: `false` makes every SUI coin an input of the PTB, which leaves
none to pay gas with on an account whose SUI is all coins (a node refuses gas
selection then). Set it `false` under `Tx.sponsored`, where the gas coin
belongs to the sponsor.

**Never fails; the recipe throws only if the SDK rejects an input, which `Tx.build` reports as `BuildError`.**

### `sign` (const)

```ts
declare const sign: (built: Built, signer: Signer) => Effect.Effect<Signed, SigningError, never>
```

Signs built bytes.

The signer's address must be the transaction's sender or, for a sponsored
transaction, its gas owner; anything else is a `SigningError` rather than a
rejection at execution time. A `Signer.remote` therefore has to report the
address it signs as truthfully.

**Fails with: `SigningError`.**

### `sponsored` (const)

```ts
declare const sponsored: (opts: {
    readonly sender: SuiAddress;
    readonly gasOwner: SuiAddress;
}) => (recipe: Recipe) => Recipe
```

Turns a recipe into a sponsored one: the sender owns the transaction, the
gas owner pays, and gas comes from the sponsor's address balance rather than
from coin objects, so the two parties never have to agree on a gas coin and
can sign in either order.

**The gas owner must hold SUI in its address balance**, not just in coin
objects: gas is drawn from that balance, and `Tx.build` refuses with
`BuildError` when it holds less than the budget. A sponsor funded by a
faucet holds coins; move SUI into the balance once with
`0x2::balance::send_funds<0x2::sui::SUI>`, or sponsor with coins instead by
passing `gasOwner` to `Tx.run`/`Tx.build` without `Tx.sponsored` (the gas
owner's coins are then selected under its sender lock). `Tx.foldCoins` with
the sponsor's signer deposits its coins.

A sponsored transaction with no owned input pins nothing — its payment is
empty, so it carries no coin and no coin reservation — and `Tx.run` sends it
without holding either party's lock past the build. It is in flight on both
parties until it settles, so the sponsor's own `tx.gas` runs wait for it
before they build (one that starts building first is not stopped by it).

**Never fails; the recipe it returns throws only if the recipe it wraps does, which `Tx.build` reports as `BuildError`.**

### `submit` (const)

```ts
declare const submit: (signed: Signed) => Effect.Effect<Executed, SubmitError, Sui>
```

Sends signed bytes, and does not stop caring until it knows what happened.

Before the first `executeTransaction` it writes a `Signed` journal entry, so
a process that dies mid-flight leaves a record of bytes that may be on the
wire. A retryable transport failure or a timeout re-sends the identical bytes
on `SubmitConfig.resubmit`; nothing is ever rebuilt, so a retry can only land
the transaction that was already signed. When the retries run out it runs
`Tx.reconcile`, which either finds the transaction, proves it never applied,
or says it does not know.

`TransportError` almost never escapes: once bytes may have been sent, "the
network was unreachable" is not an answer a caller can act on, so it becomes
`SubmissionUnknown` carrying the signed bytes. **The one exception is a node
that refused the request outright** — gRPC `INVALID_ARGUMENT`, which is what
a validator answers for malformed bytes or for a sponsored transaction
carrying one signature. That answer came from the node, it is final, and
nothing was executed, so it is reported as the `TransportError` it is rather
than reconciled: a reconcile would go on to ask "is this digest on chain?",
a question about a transaction that was never sent, and a lagging or scripted
node can answer it yes. The journal entry is settled as `NotApplied
{ evidence: "refused" }`, so `Tx.reconcileAll` has nothing left to do for it.
That holds for the **first** send only: a refusal after a timeout or a
retryable failure says nothing about the earlier send, which may have
landed, so it reconciles like any other ambiguous failure.

A validator quorum's **lock conflict** — `FAILED_PRECONDITION`, "already
locked by a different transaction" — is reconciled too, and when that ends
`SubmissionUnknown` it is reconciled up to three more times,
`SubmitConfig.reconcileRecheck` apart, because the winner of the race is
usually readable a moment later and its effects are the `inputConsumed`
evidence. Every `SubmissionUnknown` that comes out of a send names what the
last send said as well as what the reconcile found.

**An answer is journalled before anything can interrupt it.** The execute
attempts are interruptible; from the moment the node has answered to the
moment the journal holds that answer, they are not. An interrupt during the
visibility wait afterwards still wins — the caller asked for it — but the
journal already says `Executed`, and a warning says so. An execute answer for
a digest other than the submitted one is not trusted; it reconciles.

`JournalError` can only come from the `Signed` write, before anything has
been sent. Once the network has answered, a journal write that fails is
logged with `Effect.logError` and the answer stands, because "the journal is
broken" is not a thing a caller can act on and reporting it in place of a
charged `ExecutionFailed` would invite a second submission.

**Fails with: `ExecutionFailed` (applied on chain and failed; gas was charged), `NotApplied` (provably never applied), `SubmissionUnknown` (the outcome is not known and the bytes are in the error), `JournalError` (only before the first send), `TransportError` (only `INVALID_ARGUMENT` on the first send: the node refused the submission and nothing was executed).**

### `submitVia` (const)

```ts
declare const submitVia: <E, R>(signed: Signed, send: (bytes: Uint8Array, signatures: ReadonlyArray<Signature>) => Effect.Effect<SubmitViaReply, E, R>) => Effect.Effect<Executed, SubmitViaError | E, Sui | R>
```

Submits through **someone else** — a relay, a sponsorship service, a backend
that holds the only key allowed to talk to the node — and keeps the journal
and the evidence rules that `Tx.submit` would have kept.

The bytes never reach `executeTransaction` here; `send` does whatever the
service needs (an HTTP POST, a queue, another process) and answers with
whatever the service returns. What this owns is everything around it:

- a `Signed` journal entry is written **before** `send` is called, so a crash
  between here and the service leaves the same record a direct submit leaves;
- `send` is called **once**. A third party's submit is not known to be
  idempotent and re-sending is not this function's decision;
- the reply is turned into an `Executed` when it carries one (see
  `SubmitViaReply`), and otherwise the chain is asked — by the digest
  of the bytes that were handed over, which a co-signature does not change;
- a failure from `send` is **ambiguous** unless it says otherwise, so it ends
  in `Tx.reconcile` with the full evidence rules: the ordered expiry check,
  the chain-identity guard, the versioned consumer check. A sender error that
  **declares** `outcome: "not_applied"` on the instance — a 400 from the
  service, a refusal before anything went out — is taken at its word and
  fails straight through without spending a reconcile, and the journal entry
  is settled as `NotApplied { evidence: "refused" }`. A `TransportError` is
  not that: the taxonomy calls it `not_applied`, but a transport failure
  *talking to the relay* is exactly the ambiguous case, so it reconciles;
- every terminal answer is journalled, exactly as `Tx.submit` journals it.

**Fails with: `ExecutionFailed`, `NotApplied`, `SubmissionUnknown` (carrying the signed bytes, with the sender's failure as its `cause`), `JournalError` (only from the write before `send`), and `E` — whatever `send` fails with — when that error declares `outcome: "not_applied"`.**

### `verify` (const)

```ts
declare const verify: (built: Built, intent: Intent) => Effect.Effect<Verified, TransportError | SimulationFailed | BuildError | PolicyDenied, Sui>
```

Checks built bytes against a declared intent, before anything is signed.

One simulate of exactly `built.bytes`, then two checkers. The claims are
compared with the simulated effects exactly, in code, deny by default (gas
is never an outflow, whether a gas coin or the payer's address balance paid
it; an address-balance withdrawal sent to someone is). Then `Gate.screen`,
when the gate in force has one, reads the summary against the effects and
the claims rendered as words, and `Gate`'s thresholds turn its
probabilities into a refusal. With no screen configured the claims check
stands alone and the verdict is `Unscreened`. `SubmitConfig.preflight` is
not run here; `Tx.run` runs it on the same simulate.

The answer is structurally a `Built`, so it goes straight to `Tx.sign`. The
explicit lifecycle is not gated: `Tx.sign` takes any `Built`, so a caller
assembling `build`/`sign`/`submit` by hand calls this itself.

**What it does not prove.** A simulation is one execution against the state
the node had; a package that branches on a shared object, the clock or
randomness can behave differently when the bytes land. The claims bound what
was simulated, not what will execute.

Every refusal is a `PolicyDenied` whose `rule` says which check:
`intent.invalid`, `intent.outflow`, `intent.recipient`, `intent.object`,
`gate.unavailable`, `intent.injection`, `intent.vague`, `intent.effect`,
`intent.claim`.

**Fails with: `PolicyDenied`, `SimulationFailed`, `BuildError`, `TransportError`.**

### `verifySponsored` (const)

```ts
declare const verifySponsored: (built: Built, expect: {
    readonly sender: string;
    readonly gasOwner?: string;
    readonly transactionKind?: Uint8Array;
}) => Effect.Effect<Built, PolicyDenied, never>
```

Checks that sponsored bytes a service assembled are the transaction the
sender asked to have sponsored, before the sender signs them.

A sponsorship service (Enoki's `createSponsoredTransaction`, or an
application's own sponsor) takes a transaction **kind** and answers full
bytes with its gas data added. Those bytes are what the sender's signature
will authorize, so they are read back, not trusted:

- the sender the bytes name is `expect.sender` (`sponsored.sender`);
- the bytes name a gas owner, it is not the sender, and it is
  `expect.gasOwner` when one is given (`sponsored.gasOwner`);
- when `expect.transactionKind` is given, the bytes' transaction kind is
  **exactly** those kind bytes (`sponsored.kind`): a service cannot add a
  command, swap an input or change an argument and still get a signature.

The chain is `Tx.fromBytes`'s check, which made the `Built`. Simulating the
bytes before signing them is still the caller's (or `Tx.prepare`'s) job.

**Fails with: `PolicyDenied` (rule `sponsored.sender`, `sponsored.gasOwner` or `sponsored.kind`).**

## `@unconfirmed/sui-effect/journal`


6 exported symbols.

### `Journal` (const)

Re-exported from `@unconfirmed/sui-effect/tx`.

### `JournalKeyValueStoreOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface JournalKeyValueStoreOptions {
    /**
     * `"fail"` refuses to build the layer while the store holds unresolved
     * entries, which is what a service that must never silently drop a
     * transaction wants: it forces the operator (or a `Tx.reconcileAll` step
     * ahead of this layer) to settle them first. `"ignore"` builds anyway and
     * leaves them for `Tx.reconcileAll`.
     */
    readonly onUnresolved: "fail" | "ignore";
    /** A prefix for every key, so several journals can share one store. */
    readonly prefix?: string;
}
```

What a durable journal does about entries left over from a previous run.

### `JournalService` (interface)

Re-exported from `@unconfirmed/sui-effect/tx`.

### `layerKeyValueStore` (const)

```ts
declare const layerKeyValueStore: (options: JournalKeyValueStoreOptions) => Layer.Layer<never, JournalError, KeyValueStore.KeyValueStore>
```

The durable journal as a layer.

**Fails with: `JournalError` when the store cannot be read, and — with `onUnresolved: "fail"` — when the store still holds entries no one has settled.**

### `makeKeyValueStore` (const)

```ts
declare const makeKeyValueStore: (store: KeyValueStore.KeyValueStore, options?: {
    readonly prefix?: string;
}) => JournalService
```

A journal backed by a `KeyValueStore`.

Each entry is one key, `entry:<digest>`, holding the entry as JSON. A
terminal entry (`Executed`, `Failed`, `NotApplied`) overwrites the
unresolved one and stays, so a later `get` still finds the answer.

**A settled marker beside each terminal entry.** `settled:<digest>` (value:
the tag) exists only while the entry under that digest is terminal, and
`listUnresolved` reads every entry key **except** the marked ones: one
`keys` and one `get` per unresolved entry, however many settled ones the
store keeps. The marker is a negative index — it says what may be skipped,
never what must be read — so the write order is chosen to make a crash cost
a spurious read and never a missing entry:

- an unresolved `put` (`Signed`, `Unknown`) clears the marker, writes the
  entry, and clears the marker again (each clear a `has`, and a `remove`
  only when it exists). A crash after the first clear leaves an unmarked
  terminal entry, which the next listing reads once and marks.
- a terminal `put` writes the entry, then the marker. A crash between them
  leaves an unmarked terminal entry, read once and marked.
- a listing that reads an unmarked terminal entry marks it, reads the entry
  again and takes the marker back if the entry is no longer terminal — the
  other half of the second clear above, so a listing racing a fresh `Signed`
  for the same digest cannot leave it hidden. Marker writes during a listing
  are best effort and never fail it.

Nothing is read-modify-written: two fibers, two layer instances, two
processes over one store do not lose each other's entries. Two writers that
put the **same** digest at once still race, and the last `set` wins; the
lifecycle never does that, because one submission is one fiber.

**A store 0.4 wrote needs no migration.** It has entries and no markers, and
an unmarked entry is always read, so the first listing is the 0.4 scan and
marks every settled entry it finds; the second reads only the unresolved
ones. Rolling back to 0.4 is safe: 0.4 reads only `entry:` keys and ignores
the markers. **Running a 0.4 writer beside this one is not**, in one case: a
0.4 process that writes an unresolved entry over a digest this journal had
settled leaves the marker in place, and the entry is hidden from
`listUnresolved` (not from `get`). The lifecycle re-signs a settled digest
only when the same bytes are submitted again; drain with `Tx.reconcileAll`
and stop the 0.4 writers before mixing versions.

A store written by 0.3 or earlier also holds an `index` key: dead data here
— never read, never rewritten, never an error. **Rolling back to 0.3 is not
safe as is.** 0.3 lists unresolved entries only through that index, so a
`Signed` or `Unknown` entry this journal wrote is invisible to a 0.3
`listUnresolved`, `Tx.reconcileAll` and `onUnresolved: "fail"` (only
`Tx.recorded(digest)` finds it), and a 0.3 reader cannot decode a
`NotApplied { evidence: "refused" }` entry at all. Before rolling back, or
while 0.3 and later processes share a store, drain it with
`Tx.reconcileAll()` (or settle the entries by hand) and delete the
`"refused"` entries.

`keys` is still O(n) in the prefix on every store, and some stores pay for
it in values (`chrome.storage` without `getKeys`); `pruneSettled` is
how a long-lived service keeps n short.

Every member fails with `JournalError` and nothing else.

### `pruneSettled` (const)

```ts
declare const pruneSettled: (before: DateTime.DateTime, options?: {
    readonly prefix?: string;
} | undefined) => Effect.Effect<number, JournalError, KeyValueStore.KeyValueStore>
```

Deletes every **settled** entry (`Executed`, `Failed`, `NotApplied`) whose
answer was recorded strictly before `before`, and answers how many it
deleted. `Signed` and `Unknown` entries are never deleted, however old.

`listUnresolved` reads only unmarked entries, but `keys` still enumerates
every one, and some stores pay for that in values; this is what keeps the
store short in a service that runs for months. It reads every entry itself
(it deletes nothing on the strength of a marker alone), removes each pruned
entry's marker before the entry, and removes markers whose entry is gone.
After it, `Journal.get` and `Tx.recorded` answer `None` for a pruned
digest, so choose `before` older than anything you still look up by digest —
and older than any bytes you might still hand to `Tx.submit` again, because a
resubmission puts a fresh `Signed` entry that a concurrent prune of the same
digest could delete. `Tx.build` bounds a transaction to two epochs, so a
cutoff of a few days is far outside that window.

It reads the same `KeyValueStore` the journal layer was built over; pass the
same `prefix`. The dead `index` key a 0.3 journal kept is not touched.

**Fails with: `JournalError`, when the store cannot be listed, read or written, or holds an entry that does not decode (nothing is deleted on the strength of an entry it could not read).**

### `Keystore` (class)

```ts
export declare class Keystore extends Keystore_base {
    /**
     * The service over the `KeyValueStore` in context, for an application that
     * builds its own layer. No floor on `iterations`. Never fails.
     */
    static readonly make: (options?: KeystoreOptions) => Effect.Effect<KeystoreService, never, KeyValueStore.KeyValueStore>;
    /**
     * The keystore over the `KeyValueStore` in context. Builds without reading
     * anything.
     *

     * least `MIN_LAYER_ITERATIONS` — a configuration mistake that would
     * make every record cheap to brute-force. Otherwise never fails.
     */
    static readonly layer: (options?: KeystoreOptions) => Layer.Layer<Keystore, never, KeyValueStore.KeyValueStore>;
    /**
     * An in-memory keystore (`KeyValueStore.layerMemory`) sealing with 1 000
     * PBKDF2 iterations, so a test does not spend seconds per seal. **Not for
     * production**: those records are cheap to brute-force. Never fails.
     */
    static readonly layerTest: Layer.Layer<Keystore>;
}
```

The sealed keystore: `Keystore.layer(options?)` over a `KeyValueStore`,
`Keystore.layerTest` in memory for tests.

### `KeystoreAccount` (interface)

```ts

    readonly address: SuiAddress;
    readonly scheme: KeyScheme;
    /** Whether the account was derived from a recovery phrase (exportable) or imported as a key. */

```

What the keystore says about an account. Never carries secret material.

### `KeystoreError` (class)

```ts
export declare class KeystoreError extends KeystoreError_base {
    readonly message: string;
    readonly reason: "unavailable" | "wrongPassword" | "invalidPassword" | "notFound" | "exists" | "noMnemonic" | "corrupt" | "unsupported" | "storage";
    readonly address?: SuiAddress | undefined;
    readonly cause?: unknown;
    /** Nothing was sent. */
    readonly outcome: Outcome;
}
```

A keystore operation failed. Nothing was sent: outcome `not_applied`.

A wrong password is this error with `reason: "wrongPassword"`, never a
defect. `message` never contains the password or anything sealed.

### `KeystoreErrorReason` (const)

```ts
declare const KeystoreErrorReason: Schema
// decodes to: "unavailable" | "wrongPassword" | "invalidPassword" | "notFound" | "exists" | "noMnemonic" | "corrupt" | "unsupported" | "storage"
```

Why a keystore operation failed.

- `wrongPassword`: the password does not open this account's record (its
  key-check value does not match).
- `invalidPassword`: a new password is empty.
- `notFound`: no record for this address.
- `exists`: a record for this address is already stored (`import*`).
- `noMnemonic`: `exportMnemonic` of an account imported from a secret key.
- `corrupt`: the record does not decode, was moved onto another address, was
  edited (the AES-GCM tag over the ciphertext and its bound metadata does not
  verify under a password whose check value matched), or holds a key that is
  not the address's key.
- `unsupported`: the record was written by a newer format version, or names
  a KDF or cipher this version does not know.
- `unavailable`: the platform has no WebCrypto (`crypto.subtle` or
  `crypto.getRandomValues`), which React Native lacks without a polyfill.
- `storage`: the `KeyValueStore` failed; `cause` is its error.

### `KeystoreOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface KeystoreOptions {
    /** The `KeyValueStore` key prefix. Default `DEFAULT_PREFIX`. */
    readonly prefix?: string;
    /**
     * PBKDF2-SHA256 iterations for records sealed from now on (existing records
     * keep their own until `changePassword`). Default `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS`;
     * `Keystore.layer` refuses fewer than `MIN_LAYER_ITERATIONS`.
     */
    readonly iterations?: number;
}
```

Options of `Keystore.layer` and `Keystore.make`.

### `KeystoreService` (interface)

```ts
export interface KeystoreService {
    /**
     * Every account, oldest first. Reads no secret and needs no password.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`storage`; `corrupt` or `unsupported` when a
     * record does not decode as a version this release reads).
     */
    readonly list: Effect.Effect<ReadonlyArray<KeystoreAccount>, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * One account's public description.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`notFound`, `corrupt`, `unsupported`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly account: (address: SuiAddress) => Effect.Effect<KeystoreAccount, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * A fresh recovery phrase (`Signer.generateMnemonic`), its account on the
     * standard path sealed under `password`. Answers the phrase once, redacted,
     * for the person to write down.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeyError` (`unavailable`), `KeystoreError` (`invalidPassword`,
     * `exists`, `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly generate: (password: Redacted.Redacted<string>, options?: FromMnemonicOptions & {
        readonly words?: MnemonicLength;
        readonly label?: string;
    }) => Effect.Effect<{
        readonly account: KeystoreAccount;
        readonly mnemonic: Redacted.Redacted<string>;
    }, KeyError | KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * Checks a recovery phrase (`Signer.fromMnemonic`'s rules: word list and
     * checksum), derives it and seals the key and the phrase under `password`.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeyError` (`invalidMnemonic`, `invalidPath`,
     * `unsupportedScheme`), `KeystoreError` (`invalidPassword`, `exists`,
     * `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly importMnemonic: (phrase: Redacted.Redacted<string> | string, password: Redacted.Redacted<string>, options?: FromMnemonicOptions & {
        readonly label?: string;
    }) => Effect.Effect<KeystoreAccount, KeyError | KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * Seals an imported secret key (`Signer.fromSecretKey`'s spellings) under
     * `password`. The caller's bytes are copied and left as they were.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeyError` (`invalidSecretKey`, `unsupportedScheme`),
     * `KeystoreError` (`invalidPassword`, `exists`, `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly importSecretKey: (secret: Redacted.Redacted<string> | string | Uint8Array, password: Redacted.Redacted<string>, options?: FromSecretKeyOptions & {
        readonly label?: string;
    }) => Effect.Effect<KeystoreAccount, KeyError | KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * Opens an account into a `LockableSigner`. The caller locks it
     * (`signer.lock`), or acquires it with `Signer.scoped` so the scope does.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`wrongPassword`, `notFound`, `corrupt`,
     * `unsupported`, `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly unlock: (address: SuiAddress, password: Redacted.Redacted<string>) => Effect.Effect<LockableSigner, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * Deletes an account's record. Needs no password: it destroys, it reveals
     * nothing.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`notFound`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly remove: (address: SuiAddress) => Effect.Effect<void, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * Re-seals one account under a new password, with a fresh salt and IV and
     * the layer's current iteration count.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`wrongPassword`, `invalidPassword`,
     * `notFound`, `corrupt`, `unsupported`, `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly changePassword: (address: SuiAddress, oldPassword: Redacted.Redacted<string>, newPassword: Redacted.Redacted<string>) => Effect.Effect<void, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * The recovery phrase of a mnemonic account, redacted.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`noMnemonic` for an imported key,
     * `wrongPassword`, `notFound`, `corrupt`, `unsupported`, `unavailable`,
     * `storage`).
     */
    readonly exportMnemonic: (address: SuiAddress, password: Redacted.Redacted<string>) => Effect.Effect<Redacted.Redacted<string>, KeystoreError>;
    /**
     * The account's secret key as a Bech32 `suiprivkey1…`, redacted.
     *
     * Fails with: `KeystoreError` (`wrongPassword`, `notFound`, `corrupt`,
     * `unsupported`, `unavailable`, `storage`).
     */
    readonly exportSecretKey: (address: SuiAddress, password: Redacted.Redacted<string>) => Effect.Effect<Redacted.Redacted<string>, KeystoreError>;
}
```

```ts
export interface IndexedDbOpenRequest extends IndexedDbRequest<IndexedDbDatabase> {
    onupgradeneeded: ((event: never) => void) | null;
    onblocked: ((event: never) => void) | null;
}
```

The part of an `IDBOpenDBRequest` this adapter reads.

### `IndexedDbOptions` (interface)

```ts
export interface IndexedDbOptions {
    /** The factory to open the database with. Defaults to `globalThis.indexedDB`. */
    readonly indexedDB?: IndexedDbFactory;
    /** The database name. Defaults to `"sui-effect"`. */
    readonly databaseName?: string;
    /** The object store name. Defaults to `"keyvalue"`. */
    readonly storeName?: string;
    /**
     * The durability hint every write transaction carries. Defaults to
     * `"strict"`: the journal's `Signed` entry is written before any bytes are
     * sent, and a write that a power loss can undo is not a journal. Browsers
     * that do not know the hint ignore it.
     */
    readonly durability?: "default" | "strict" | "relaxed";
}
```

Options for `layerIndexedDb`.

### `IndexedDbRequest` (interface)

```ts
export interface IndexedDbRequest<T> {
    readonly result: T;
    readonly error: unknown;
    onsuccess: ((event: never) => void) | null;
    onerror: ((event: never) => void) | null;
}
```

The part of an `IDBRequest` this adapter reads.

### `IndexedDbTransaction` (interface)

```ts

    readonly error: unknown;
    objectStore(name: string): IndexedDbObjectStore;
    abort(): void;
    oncomplete: ((event: never) => void) | null;
    onerror: ((event: never) => void) | null;
```
